  The AiffWriter will write the audio signal to an AIFF file at the point the AiffWriter plugin is inserted at.
  The Dac output sample rate affects the overall audio fidelity of RWAudioCore output. Higher sample rates mean higher quality, but more CPU usage.
  The Dac plugin is responsible for taking the RWAudioCore mixer output and ultimately getting it out the speakers so the user can hear the output of RWAudioCore.
  The Dac plugin is special in that it is typically the only true output plugin. This means it actually drives the RWAudioCore mixing process. The Dac plugin will hook itself into some kind of periodic update from a self created thread, or hook into a callback from a lower level audio system. It will then periodically determine how many samples need to be mixed and then call into the RWAudioCore mixer. It will then format the RWAudioCore mixer output appropriately and send it off to the lower level audio system.
  The Dac plugin should typically only ever be inserted on the mastering voice. It should always be inserted as the last plugin on the voice. There should also be only one Dac plugin active in the entire RWAudioCore system. The management of the Dac plugin should typically be done by game code, not by data driven audio tools.
  The Dac supports a standard set of sample rates; 22050, 24000, 32000, 44100, 48000, 96000, and 192000. The actual sample rates supported on each platform will be a subset of these. Xbox 360 currently only supports 48000 and Wii currently only supports 32000 for example.
  The GainFader plug-in provides a mechanism for changing the audio signal gain at a specified start time, over some time period, and according to some amplitude change characteristic. A start time for the change is specified in seconds. This requires the plug-in to determine the current system time (via the GetTime() system call), and specify the start time based on that. For example, if the desired start time is 1 second from the current time, 1 would be added to the current time and specified as the start time. A start time of 0.0 will always cause the fade to start immediately. A fade time is also specified in seconds, and denotes the duration of the amplitude change. This change (or fade) will then occur over the fade time duration to smoothly arrive at the specified final gain value, given by the endGain parameter. If a fade is already in progress when a new fade request is made, the new fade request will be applied starting at the most recent gain value of the fade that was in progress. Even if this new fade request has a start time in the future, any fade that is currently in progress will be stopped at the most recent gain value. If the specified start time of a fade is already in the past, but the projected end time of the fade has not yet passed, the fade is started immediately. If the projected end time is also in the past, the fade is cancelled. Currently three fade characteristics are supported; linear amplitude, linear power, and sine amplitude.
  The HighPassButterworth plug-in provides users with a high pass version of the classic analog IIR Butterworth filter. The Butterworth filter frequency response is referred to as being maximally flat in the passband. What this means in practice is that the pass band frequency response of the filter is very flat, not exhibiting the ripples seen in other filter types, such as the Butterworth's analog cousins, the Chebyshev and Elliptic filters. Also, the frequency response is referred to as being monotonic in the pass and stop bands, which means that as frequency decreases, the amplitude of the response never increases, but rather will either stay the same or decrease. The filter gain in the passband is normalized to 0 dB. Near the filter cutoff frequency (3 dB point), the rolloff of the filter will be around 6 dB per octave per order or pole (see ATTRIBUTE_SETFREQUENCY description). As such, users can select the filter order based on their needs. For example, if the use case requires a filter with maximum rolloff in order to cut out any frequencies below a certain value, the maximum filter order should be chosen. On the other hand if the use case requires a very gentle rolloff in order to model some real world physical acoustic phenomenon, then perhaps selecting a lower order makes the most sense. These filters are guaranteed to be stable, something that is inherent in the digital Butterworth filter design process. 
  The HighPassIir2 plug-in allows for the relatively inexpensive high pass filtering of a signal. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a transition band roll-off of at least 12 dB per octave.The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear; though the degree of non-linearity in the pass band is not severe. Filter stability is assured by clamping the cutoff frequency (internally) to always be BETWEEN zero Hertz and the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate).
  The HighShelfIir2 plug-in is a relatively inexpensive filter that can boost or cut the signal spectrum from a selected cutoff frequency up to the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate). The filter provides up to +/- 25 dB gain in the gain region, and rolls off at approximately 12 dB per octave to a gain of 0 dB elsewhere. This 0-dB gain characteristic allows for the filter to be chained in series with other filters supporting this characteristic, since it does not attenuate or magnify the signal outside of its gain region. As such, the filter can be useful in a parametric EQ application, where it could provide what is traditionally referred to as treble control. The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear. 
  The LowPassButterworth plug-in provides users with a low pass version of the classic analog IIR Butterworth filter. The Butterworth filter frequency response is referred to as being maximally flat in the passband. What this means in practice is that the pass band frequency response of the filter is very flat, not exhibiting the ripples seen in other filter types, such as the Butterworth's analog cousins, the Chebyshev and Elliptic filters. Also, the frequency response is referred to as being monotonic in the pass and stop bands, which means that as frequency increases, the amplitude of the response never increases, but rather will either stay the same or decrease. The filter gain in the passband is normalized to 0 dB. Near the filter cutoff frequency (3 dB point), the rolloff of the filter will be around 6 dB per octave per order or pole (see ATTRIBUTE_SETFREQUENCY description). As such, users can select the filter order based on their needs. For example, if the use case requires a filter with maximum rolloff in order to cut out any frequencies beyond a certain value, the maximum filter order should be chosen. On the other hand if the use case requires a very gentle rolloff in order to model some real world physical acoustic phenomenon (such as for example occlusion), then perhaps selecting a lower order (such as 1) makes the most sense. These filters are guaranteed to be stable, somethingthat is inherent in the digital Butterworth filter design process. 
  The PacketPlayer PlugIn is a simple source plug-in used to play raw sample data by submitting it as small chucks of sample data in packets. The PacketPlayer queues up the submitted packets and plays them in FIFO sequence. When a packet has finished processing, it is considered 'finished' and it is up to theclient to deal with it after that point; finished packets don't have to be removed from PacketPlayer. To determine the processing state of a packet, use the EVENT_ISPACKETDONE event. While a packet has not finished processing, it is considered the property of the PacketPlayer and its state may not be changed.
  The Pan3D plug-in accepts a mono source as the input signal and produces a 6 channel output. If rendering of stereo or multichannel sources is required, the user will have to first split the input into multiple mono sources.
  The Pan3D plug-in allows for spatializing a mono signal over a 5.1 channel configuration. This plug-in is intended for use with the RWAudio3D library. RWAudio3D is responsible for producing a set of parameters or DSP coefficients which is to be used by Pan3D in processing the audio input signal.
  The Pan3D plug-in consists of a multi-tap delay line, a number of filters, and a number of Vector Base Amplitude Panning (VBAP) panners. The length of the delay line is determined by the constructor parameter maxDelaySeconds, and the number of read taps is determined by the number of reflections (a parameter in the DSPSettings struct) plus 1 for the direct path. The positions of the read taps are determined by the delay values of the direct and reflected paths. The input audio signal is first read either directly from a tap, or via interpolation in the case of a changing delay and corresponding Doppler shift. It then passes through a low pass filter, followed by a low shelving filter in the case of the direct path only, and finally into an improved VBAP panner. The low pass filter simulates air and surface absorption effects while the low shelving filter models the proximity effect. The VBAP panner maps the direct path azimuth to gain values for each of the 5 main channels in the 5.1 setup to allow for a smooth panning transition when the sound emitter is very close to the listener. The reflected paths use 2 channel panning on the assumption that reflections are normally far away, thereby reducing the amount of memory needed.
  The Pause plugin provides a means for audio processing of source plugins to be paused.
  The ReverbIR1 plug-in provides an efficient method for applying the reverberation characteristic of a given space to the game audio signal. This is achieved by performing the fast convolution of the signal with the impulse response (captured or created) of a given reverberant space. The efficient implementation is achieved by performing the convolution in the frequency domain, after having first sectioned the impulse response into smaller pieces in order to minimize delay and memory usage. To apply a given impulse response, a given impulse response file (such as say a stereo .wav file that contains the IR) must first be converted to an irf file (via SIMEX/SX). This file must be at the same sample rate as the output mode used by the game. The response is then applied to the signal via the EVENT_APPLYREVERB event. While the PlugIn has been designed for efficient operation, it does still require considerable resources, especially memory. To help mitigate this, memory usage can be traded off against the high frequency content of the reverberated signal retained. This is specified when creating the impulse response file (irf) via the SIMEX/SX parameter percentHighFrequencyCut. Reductions in memory usage will be nearly proportional to the numberspecified (i.e., if 50.0 is specified, memory usage will go down by close to half). To also minimize cycle usage, the SIMEX/SX parameter samplesPerFFTBlock allows for specification of the FFT block (segment)size. Larger sizes mean more efficient processing, but also add delay. Sizes used by the FFT must be a power of 2, so any sizes specified that aren't will be forced to a power of 2. The ReverbIR1 plug-in supports several channel-impulse response file configurations. 1:1 source to output channel mappings (1, 2, 4, or 6 channels) are supported, where the impulse response file is either mono, or it is comprised of the same number of channels as the signal input. Also, a 1:n source to output channel configuration is supported (1, 2, 4, or 6 output channels), where the impulse response file is comprised of n channels.
  The ReverbModel1 plug-in is a parameterized model-based reverberator that allows some user control of the size and characteristics of a reverberant space.  Each instance takes in a single (mono) source, and fans out a reverberated spatialized signal to the selected number of output channels (except the LFE in 6 channel case). The reverberator is comprised of 6 parallel comb filters followed by a series allpass filter. A degree of spatialization is achieved by using slightly different parameters for the allpass on one output channel versus another. Control over the reverb is achieved via 3 parameters: the reverb space size, reverberation time, and brightness. Each of these is associated with an attribute, and described there.
  The Send plugin allows the signal to be bussed out to the specified submix input of another voice. A gain may be applied to control the mix level going into the submix. The number of output channels from the Send does not have to match the number of inputs to the SubMix, if they do not match, a fold-down or fold-up will occur. Sends and SubMixes will disconnect from each other safely and automatically if either disappear due to their voices being destroyed/etc.
  The Snd9Service PlugIn executes the SND9 legacy system internals and bridges the output of the SND9 mixer into an RWAudioCore Voice.
  The SndPlayer1 PlugIn is the main player used for game audio sample playback. It efficiently supports resident RAM samples, streamed samples, gigastream samples, stitched playback, looped samples, sample accurate starting, up to 64 channels, and multiple compression types.
  The SubMix PlugIn behaves like a mix bin. Any number of voices may connect their output via a Send or Route PlugIn to a SubMix. The SubMix will mix the output of these and pass the result down the voice it is plugged into.
  The SubMix is a "source" PlugIn, and it must be inserted alone at position 0 on the voice. The SubMix's input channels must be set to 0, and the output channels must set to either 1, 2, 4, or 6 channels.
  The TimeStretch plugin provides the capability for users to stretch or shorten a sound while preserving its pitch and spectral characteristics. This is in contrast to time stretching by means of resampling, which changes the pitch of the sound in order to change its length. On the other hand, the TimeStretch plugin can also be used in conjunction with a Resample plugin to achieve a time-invariant pitch shift. For example, a sound could be pitched up a third octave (~= 26%) with the length preserved by stretching it by a factor of 1.26 using the time stretcher combined with pitching it up with the Resample plugin by the same factor of 1.26.
  The TimeStretch plugin supports single and multi-channel operation, even though it is expected that most speech sources being stretched will be single channel. In the case of multi-channel operation, the user can select between MULTICHANNELMODE_INDEPENDENT_CHANNELS and MULTICHANNELMODE_COMBINED_CHANNELS modes at voice contruction. These modes differ only in how the correlation points are found for each of the channels. Since this analysis operation is expensive, it is possible to use a kind of mean best correlation point across all channels and thereby save doing the analysis on each channel. This corresponds to the MULTICHANNELMODE_COMBINED_CHANNELS mode, and results in only a slightly higher processing load than what would be experienced in processing a single channel. This mode works well if the different channels all still have a relatively high degree of correlation with each other, such as would be the case if they were all derived from the same speech source, but perhaps have had different effects applied to them, such as different types or amounts of reverberation, filtering, or background noise. In these cases, this mode might also be preferred over the independent channel mode (described below) due to it keeping the stretching and contracting of all channels synchronized. This synchronization occurs because the cut/loop points are identical for all channels, and so all channels will loop or cut at the same times.
  The TimeStretch plugin uses a time domain based approach to altering the length of a sound. This is in contrast to frequency domain based approaches that must first convert the signal to the frequency domain (typically via the FFT). Both approaches have advantages and disadvantages associated with them, and there is no single ideal time stretching method that works well on all types of audio content in all processing situations. This particular time stretcher is designed to work well in the stretching and contraction of speech content, typically with stretch ratios in the range of 0.75 to 1.5. In contrast to a frequency domain based approach, it achieves this with less or no chorusing and phasing artifacts, no transient smearing, and a much lighter processing load.
  The block of memory pointed to must not be modified while the sample is being played. If a SndPlayer1 instance is actively playing a sample or has a sample queued and it wishes to free up the block of memory it sits it, it is not sufficient to simply call the SndPlayer1's Stop event or the PlugIn's Release() method and then immediately remove the sample data. The Stop event is asynchronous, therefore the sample data will continue to be referenced by RWAudioCore for a short period of time after the call to Stop. To ensure the sample data is no longer in use, wait until the Stop command has been processed. This can be guaranteed by using the rw::audio::core::System::IsCommandComplete() mechanism.
  The current implementation of the Pan3D plug-in uses the IIR2 class for both the low pass and the low shelving filter, and uses CopyWithGainRamp() and MixWithGainRamp() to apply the gain matrices. The delay line interpolation is implemented as a two-point linear interpolation. In the case where the sound source is located at such a great distance that the size of the delay line required exceeds maxDelaySeconds and the user still requires the Doppler effect, the Pan3D plug-in will use a time-invariant implementation of a pitch shifter which is based on crossfading two modulated delay lines. 
  The offline-mix-frame event can only be issued when the Dac has been started in offline mode.  The offline-mix-frame event processes immediately, and causes the Dac to execute all commands in the command queue and produce a single mix frame.  Note that when the Dac is in offline mode, the mix frames that are produced are not sent to the audio hardware.
  The only parameter under the user's control during operation of the plugin is the stretch ratio (ATTRIBUTE_STRETCHRATIO). This sets the factor or ratio by which the signal is stretched. A value of less than 1 results in contraction of the signal, while a value of greater than 1 results in elongation. The theoretical maximum stretch ratio that can be achieved with this algorithm is 2.0, and the minimum ratio (contraction) is 0.5. As such, this is the range of allowed values that may be specified by the user. However, in practice, the mean maximum range that will be achievable over time for a typical audio signal will be 0.625 to 1.75. Furthermore, in MULTICHANNELMODE_INDEPENDENT_CHANNELS mode, in order to avoid potentially large disparities in the amount of buffer required by one channel versus another, the stretch ratio is internally limited to the minimum range possible for any signal at any time. This corresponds to a range of 0.75 to 1.5.
  The output mode should ideally be set before the Dac is started. However it can be changed while the Dac is running without any side effects to the RWAudioCore code or game code. The audio output will be interrupted for a brief period, which can sound disconcerting. If this is an issue the signal on the mastering voice could be faded down and back around the switch by manipulating a fader plugin appropriately.
  The parameter should point to a C style string which matches the name of a SubMix that is currently instantiated. This is a sound artist friendly method for connecting to named submixes as names can be embedded in the sound assets at creation time. For example, a submix can be named "Reverb". If the send will always connect to the reverb engine, it can connect via the "Reverb" name. The most common use will be to connect to the "Master" SubMix.
  The play event starts the RawPuller plugin, allowing samples to be played via the user callback.  The user is responsible for providing the data requested, guarantee playback kickoff accuracy (insert silence at the start if necessary), playback declicking, and track playback progress themselves. 
  The sample rate parameter determines the sample rate of the audio that will be submitted.
  The sample rate should ideally be set before the Dac is started. However it can be changed while the Dac is running with minimal side effects to the RWAudioCore code or game code. The audio output will be interrupted for a brief period, which can sound disconcerting. If this is an issue the signal on the mastering voice could be faded down and back around the switch by manipulating a fader plugin appropriately. Note that pitch sensitive plugins must adjust to the new output sample rate on the fly. Most pitch sensitive plugins will handle this automatically, but some pitch sensitive plugins will choose not to handle this automatically as doing so could lead to large inefficiencies. For example, a convolution reverb requires an impulse response recorded at the same sample rate as the output sample rate. Switching the output sample rate dynamically causes the reverb engine to not be using the correct impulse response. In that case the reverb plugin must be stopped before the sample rate switch, and then started again with the correct impulse response after the sample rate switch.
  The set mode event allows a game to set the Dac output to a new output mode.
  The set sample rate event allows a game to set the Dac output sample rate to a new sample rate.
  The start2 event must be called in order for any RWAudioCore playback to occur. The Dac is not started upon instantiation.
  The start2 event tells the Dac plugin to begin operation.  When started in realtime mode, the Dac will begin driving the RWAudioCore mixer process and outputting the resulting sample data to the speakers.  When started in offline mode, the Dac will enter a state where one mix frame is processed for each EVENT_OFFLINEMIXFRAME event.  In offline mode, sample data is not sent to the speakers.  If you wish to use the audio data that is produced you will need to use the SampleCapture or AiffWriter plugins, or a suitable replacement.
  This attribute allows for specification of how the compressor will behave in a multi-channel case (the norm). If grouped operation is specified, the channel with the lowest current gain will have its gain applied to all channels. This will normally be the channel with the highest signal level, but given attack and release times, and the time constant of the lowpass filter doing the averaging, it could occasionally be a different channel. Use of this mode allows for the preservation of the multi-channel image, since whenever gain is applied, the gain value applied will be the same across all channels. This mode is the default. If independent mode is selected, each channel will use a gain based solely on that channels mean magnitude. This mode might be useful if the user simply wants to compress when it is warrented, on a channel-by-channel basis. 
  This attribute allows for specification of how the limiter will behave in a multi-channel case (the norm). If grouped operation is specified, the channel with the lowest current gain will have its gain applied to all channels. This will normally be the channel with the highest signal level, but given attack and release times, and the time constant of the lowpass filter doing the averaging, it could occasionally be a different channel. Use of this mode allows for the preservation of the multi-channel image, since whenever gain is applied, the gain value applied will be the same across all channels. This mode is the default. If independent mode is selected, each channel will use a gain based solely on that channels mean magnitude. This mode might be useful if the user simply wants to compress when it is warrented, on a channel-by-channel basis. 
  This attribute allows for the adjustment of the perceived brightness of the reverb. By increasing the brightness, the reverberated signal will retain proportionally higher frequency components. This is achieved by increasing the cutoff frequency of each lowpass filter in the comb filter feedback loop. By selecting a brightness of 1 (the default), the frequency content of the reverb will be matched with that defined by the model. It is recommended that this default normally be used. However, tweaking of this parameter may be useful to emulate effects such as the humidity of the environment (i.e., higher humidity corresponds to a less bright sound, since the moisture absorbs higher frequencies). 
  This attribute allows for the specification of the time in seconds over which the compressor reaches full compression, given that the mean magnitude of the input has exceeded the threshold. The punchiness characteristic of some compressors can be achieved by setting a relatively longer attack time ( > 1 second).
  This attribute allows for the specification of the time in seconds over which the compressor turns off, given that the mean magnitude of the input has dropped below the (off) threshold. The sustain of certain compressors can be achieved by a longer release time. 
  This attribute allows for the specification of the time in seconds over which the limiter turns off, given that the mean magnitude of the input has dropped below the (off) threshold. The sustain of certain limiters can be achieved by a longer release time. 
  This attribute specifies the anticipated maximum delay amount in seconds. The value can be set by the user to force the plugin to allocate sufficient memory during initialisation. It should be set to the highest value that ATTRIBUTE_SETDELAYTIME will be set to during the lifetime of the plugin.
  This attribute specifies the dB ratio between output and input signal levels when the compressor is in the on state. For example, a ratio of 5 means that, a 5 dB increase in the input signal level will result in a 1 dB increase in the output signal level. 
  This attribute specifies the filter bandwidth in Hertz. A value greater than or equal to twice the Nyquist frequency (which equals the sample rate) will always result in passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. Lesser bandwidths will also turn the filter off, if the center frequency is appropriately set. For example, if the center frequency is in the middle of the band, a bandwidth greater than or equal to the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) would be wide enough to take in the entire band, and therefore turn off the filter. A bandwidth of zero will result in the maximum amount of filtering, and will be clamped internally to something slightly larger than zero to ensure filter stability (Filter Q = frequency/bandwdith is currently limited to a maximum of 20). 
  This attribute specifies the filter bandwidth in Hertz. A value greater than or equal to twice the Nyquist frequency (which equals the sample rate) will always result in passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. Lesser bandwidths will also turn the filter off, if the center frequency is appropriately set. For example, if the center frequency is in the middle of the band, a bandwidth greater than or equal to the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) would be wide enough to take in the entire band, and therefore turn off the filter. A bandwidth of zero will result in the maximum amount of filtering. 
  This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. A value greater than the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum unmodified, and therefore the filter will be bypassed.  A value of 0 Hertz or less will be clamped to the minimal allowable frequency (very near 0 Hertz) such that the filter remains stable (poles within the unit circle). This results in the specified gain being applied to the entire spectrum.
  This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. A value greater than the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value of 0 Hertz or less will be clamped to 0 Hertz, resulting in the maximum amount of filtering.
  This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. A value greater than the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value of 0 Hertz or less will be clamped to the minimal allowable frequency (very near 0 Hertz) such that the filter remains stable (poles within the unit circle). This results in the maximum amount of filtering.
  This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. A value of 0 Hertz or less is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum unmodified, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value greater than or equal to the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) will be clamped to the maximum allowable frequency (very near the Nyquist frequency) such that the filter remains stable (poles within the unit circle). This results in the specified gain being applied to the entire spectrum.
  This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. A value of 0 Hertz or less is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value greater than or equal to the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) will be clamped to the maximum allowable frequency (very near the Nyquist frequency) such that the filter remains stable (poles within the unit circle).   This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. A value of 0 Hertz or less is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value greater than or equal to the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate)will be clamped to the Nyquist frequency, resulting in the maximum amount of filtering.
  This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. The cutoff frequency is the frequency at which the amplitude response of the filter is 3 dB below its value in the passband (nominally 0 dB). This is also sometimes referred to as the knee of the frequency response. A value greater than the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value of 0 Hertz results in the maximum amount of filtering. Note that in order to avoid excessively long filter coefficient calculation times on some platforms (PC), ATTRIBUTE_SETFREQUENCY is clamped to stay within 1% of the Nyquist frequency at both DC at the low end of the spectrum, and the Nyquist frequency at the high end. Given an output sample rate of 48000 Hz for example, this would result in a minimum cutoff frequency of 240 Hz (1% of 48000/2), and a maximum (before the filter turns off) of 23670 Hz (48000/2 - (1% of 48000/2)). 
  This attribute specifies the filter cutoff frequency in Hertz. The cutoff frequency is the frequency at which the amplitude response of the filter is 3 dB below its value in the passband (nominally 0 dB). This is also sometimes referred to as the knee of the frequency response. A value of 0 Hz is equivalent to passing the entire spectrum, and therefore the filter will be bypassed. A value of the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate) results in the maximum amount of filtering. Note that in order to avoid excessively long filter coefficient calculation times on some platforms (PC), ATTRIBUTE_SETFREQUENCY is clamped to stay within 1% of the Nyquist frequency at both DC at the low end of the spectrum, and the Nyquist frequency at the high end. Given an output sample rate of 48000 Hz for example, this would result in a minimum cutoff frequency of 240 Hz (1% of 48000/2), and a maximum (before the filter turns off) of 23670 Hz (48000/2 - (1% of 48000/2)). 
  This attribute specifies the filter order, which is equivalent to the number of delay elements in either the feedforward or feedback paths. The number of feedback or feedforward coefficients is equal to the order + 1. A higher order filter will exhibit a steeper rolloff than a lower order one. In the case of purely feedback filters with no feedforward terms, the rolloff is generally around 6 dB per octave (doubling of frequency) per order. However the addition of feedforward terms (which the Butterworth filters have) makes the relationship between order and rolloff less straightforward. These feedforward terms contribute 'zeroes' to the transfer function response, which, for example, makes for a very steep slope as the frequency approaches the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate). Near the cutoff frequency, however, one can expect a slope of roughly 6 dB per order. 
  This attribute specifies the reverberation time in seconds. It is a measure of the time it takes the reverberation of a given sound to decay. Longer times would, for example, tend to be associated with highly reflective walls or surfaces. A value of 0 results in turning the reverb off, and stopping audio output from the associated voice. The minimum non-zero reverb time is 0.366 seconds, and values between this and 0 are clamped to it (0.366). 
  This attribute specifies the size of the reverberant space via the setting of the average linear dimension of that space (in meters). The simplest example is that of a square room, in which case the space size would be specified as the distance between opposite walls. More complex geometries should be estimated by determining the average distance between pairs of opposing reflecting surfaces. 
  This attribute specifies the value in dB that the signal mean magnitude must reach before the compressor turns on. An off threshold that is slightly less than this, to achieve some hysteresis, is set automatically to trigger turning the compressor off. Since this compressor threshold must be exceeded by the signal mean magnitude, in order to ensure that signal peaks that exceed the mean are also compressed, a threshold that is much lower than those peaks should be selected. A value greater than or equal to 20.0 dB will turn the compressor off completely, though any value greater than the expected local mean magnitude signal level will also result in no compression (e.g., if the mean signal level never exceeds -2.0 dB (~0.8), a threshold of 0 or -1 dB will result in the compressor always being off).
  This attribute specifies the value in dB that the signal mean magnitude must reach before the limiter turns on. An off threshold that is slightly less than this, to achieve some hysteresis, is set automatically to trigger turning the limiter off. Since this limiter threshold must be exceeded by the signal mean magnitude, in order to ensure that signal peaks that exceed the mean are also compressed, a threshold that is much lower than those peaks should be selected. A value greater than or equal to 20.0 dB will turn the limiter off completely, though any value greater than the expected local mean magnitude signal level will also result in no limiting (e.g., if the mean signal level never exceeds -2.0 dB (~0.8), a threshold of 0 or -1 dB will result in the limiter always being off).
  This event results in the DSPSettings object location being passed to Pan3D. The object is also copied to local memory owned by the Pan3D. The reason for copying the DSPSettings object to Pan3D local memory is to minimize the time duration during which the applicationcreating and filling in the DSPSettings object (such as RWAudio3D) and Pan3D are accessingthe same memory space. Given this approach, the only time during which a conflict could occur is during this event, when the data of DSPSettings is being copied over. As such, it is essential that the RWAC lock (rw::audio::core::System::Lock()) be acquired by the application whenever it is modifying the DSPSettings object. In the case of RWAudio3D, for example, this would be whenever the application calls the Calculate() method of that package to update the DSPSettings object values. 
  This event results in the impulse response pointed to in ReverbParams being applied to the input signal.
  This event will stop the Dac from driving the RWAudioCore mixer and sending output to the speakers.
  This event will stop the reverb that was applied with EVENT_APPLYREVERB, clear all resources used in the application of that particular reverb file, and stop the audio output from the associated voice.
  This parameter is a pointer to a DSPSettings object that contains all the direct and reflected path details necessary for Pan3D to render the audio input to a 3D space. Typically the DSPSetting object will have been created by a higher level 3D audio application such as RWAudio3D. The object pointed to must be minimally aligned to 16 bytes to facilitate successful DMA transfers (PS3) and efficient processing and memory handling on all platforms. 
  This parameter should point to the impulse response file loaded into memory. It is used directly by the plugin and therefore must remain in memory until either a new reverb file has been applied via the EVENT_APPLYREVERB event, the reverb has been stopped with the EVENT_STOPREVERB event, or the plugin has been released. In these cases, the impulse response file can be freed from memory. To ensure that processing is complete and the file can indeed be freed, use the rw::audio::core::System::IsCommandComplete() mechanism.
  This parameter specifies the mode in which the Dac should be started.  Possible values are STARTMODE_REALTIME and STARTMODE_OFFLINE.
  This parameter specifies the output mode to set the Dac to.
  This parameter specifies the output sample rate to set the Dac to.
  This plugin is of type PLUGINTYPE_INPUT_PROCESS. It can only reside in a source voice, which is a special voice that contains an input source at the top (PLUGINTYPE_INPUT_SOURCE), and possibly various other source voice plugins such as a resample or rechannel. The reason it can only reside in a source voice is because it relies on being able to produce a different number of output samples from the number of input samples it receives. Non-source voice plugins must produce the same number of samples as they receive.
  This plugin is primarily useful as a debugging aid. The audio signal can be captured at various points along the RWAC mixer signal paths (e.g. on the mastering voice before the Dac, or after the Resample plugin on a voice using a SndPlayer). If crackling is being introduced somewhere in the signal path the AiffWriter can be moved around until the plugin that causes the crackling is found. As the AiffWriter is of PLUGINTYPE_STANDARD, it cannot be inserted before such plugins as a SndPlayer, Rechannel, Resample, or TimeStretch.
  This value indicates that the Pause PlugIn should allow audio to play.
  This value indicates that the Pause PlugIn should pause the audio playback.
  This value indicates that the compressor should treat each channel separately when performing compression. 
  This value indicates that the compressor should treat the channels as a group, and apply the gain that is smallest across all channels at any given time. 
  This value indicates that the limiter should treat each channel separately when performing compression. 
  This value indicates that the limiter should treat the channels as a group, and apply the gain that is smallest across all channels at any given time. 
  This value indicates the Voice the SndPlayer1 PlugIn is inserted on should remain alive. The SndPlayer1 will remain available to accept more sample requests, or it will continue playing any remaining sample requests in the queue.
  This value indicates the Voice the SndPlayer1 PlugIn is inserted on will be expelled when the sample request naturally finishes playing.
  This value sets the correlation peak detection algorithm coarse correlation step size or granularity to 16 samples. This means that a coarse correlation is done for every 16th sample offset. This is the default value used, and is highly recommended, unless user testing against particular audio content has indicated a different value is required or preferable, or, if a very low output sample rate is being used (less than 44,100 Hz).
  UI/PluginBroadcasterProc
  Unlike other plugins, pitch change can only be affected on source voices with this method. This is because pitch changes affect the rate samples are requested or produced at through the signal chain. In order to keep the general purpose plugin case simple and solve all sorts of other pitch synchronization issues, RenderWare Audio Core only allows pitch changes near the top of a source voice. After that, all sample data is at a uniform sample rate which makes processing it far simpler. This restriction introduces ramifications to the various end users and other technology control layers used to drive RenderWare Audio Core. Instead of being able to simply plunk down one or more pitch controls on source and submix voices, to achieve desired effects such as singular control at a submix point and doppler, control layers must maintain a virtual mapping of all the desired control points, compute a final pitch for each source voice, and apply it.
  When a Voice is expelled, it and all the plugins on it are no longer useable, but they are not released.
  When a mode other than 5.1 is set, the Dac plugin will perform an industry standard fold-down to the quad, stereo or mono output mode. Even if the Dac is not performing the fold-down, the PC or console platform might be performing a fold-down on its end. Hard clipping can be introduced during the fold-down if the signal coming into the fold-down is loud. Although the hard clipping should not be as audible a problem as it might sound, it can be avoided by reducing the volume of the 5.1 signal before it goes into the Dac plugin. If a signal is normalized to full scale (+/- 1.0) before it enters the Dac plugin by a plugin such as a compressor (strongly suggested), then when folding down to quad reducing the signal to 58% (-4.7 dB) of it's original level will guarantee no hard clipping will occur. When folding down to stereo, 37% (-8.6 dB) will guarantee no clipping. In reality, hard clipping noise is not as noticeable as one might think, and it may also be difficult drive the 5.1 signal to produce the extreme configuration required that will give a maximum peak after fold-down. For this reason it is never suggested that the volume be reduced to entirely reduce clipping (because a lot of volume will be lost), but instead find a happy medium through common sense and experimentation (e.g. somewhere between 0 and -6 dB).
  When active, the RawPuller will issue the callback to ask users for an amount of data to process.  It is also allowed to call the START or STOP events in the callback.  Please ensure, like in other callback functions, to not spend more than a few milliseconds in this callback, or else the RWAudioCore mixer would be starved.
  When issued, the callback will tell users how many frames are needed (numSamples), and the current pitch in the mixer (pitch).  Users are responsible to obtain the actual number of samples needed by taking into account the pitch, and provide the requested numSamples number of frames via the pDest parameter. 
  When stitching, the samples being stitched can be in any format (different sample rates, compression types, number of channels), and the joining of two samples will still be seamless.
  When the START event is called, the AIFF file header is written out. The AIFF file is then appended to each time the plugin processes the sample data. When STOP is called, the AIFF file header is updated with the correct number of samples. If the AiffWriter plugin is not stopped or released gracefully, then the header will not be updated, and the AIFF file will likely appear corrupt.
  When the control is set to "paused" the Pause plugin ramps the gain to zero and then stops asking for more data from the input plugin (e.g. SndPlayer). When the pause is released the gain is ramped back up and playback continues.

  Defines the maximum delay length to be allocated for a Pan3D instance. The delay line is used for simulating the delay effect of the direct sound path from the sound source, the variously delayed reflections off walls and other surfaces, and the Doppler effect. A larger value normally allows the Pan3D instance to accommodate more reflections. If the size of the room that the Pan3D instance is simulating is large, maxDelaySeconds will also need to be large in order to enable hearing of at least all the first order reflections. A general usage guideline is to set maxDelaySeconds to the size of room that Pan3D is simulating (converted to seconds based on the speed of sound), within the constraints of available memory.

  >Setting gamma=0
  ?The Compressor1 plug-in provides a means for dynamic range reduction of the signal. When the mean magnitude of the signal exceeds some settable threshold, a gain term is applied to the signal to reduce the overall amplitude. This gain will only cut in fully after a specified attack time, and conversely, will only be fully released when the mean magnitude is below the threshold (including hysteresis) for a specified release time. The gain amount is based on a settable ratio, and can be derived on a per channel basis, or can be based on the maximum mean magnitude channel across all channels. The punchiness characteristic of some compressors can be achieved by setting a relatively longer attack time ( > 1 second), while the sustain of certain compressors can be achieved by a longer release time. It should be noted that since the compressor threshold must be exceeded by the signal mean magnitude (via a lowpass filter with very low cutoff), in order to ensure that signal peaks that far exceed the mean are also compressed, a threshold that is much lower than those peaks must be selected. This might require a certain amount of experimentation. 
  @?.x%lx.c itemconfigure l%lx -fill black
  A PS3 game that wishes to support PL2 is faced with one of two choices on how to know when to enable it. The game code could have a front end menu option to allow the user to select between stereo and PL2. This is however very ugly to the end user as they now have to configure audio output in more than one place. The option is also confusing as it doesn't force stereo or PL2 mode if the OSD is set to a 5.1 or 7.1 output mode. For these reasons a menu option is not recommended. The saner alternative is to decide up front if your game will always output either plain stereo or PL2. This would probably be the audio director's choice based on what they prefer.
  A SndPlayer1 instance will always output a constant number of channels, but the source sample data may be of any channel configuration. If the source and output channels do not map 1:1, then a standard fold-up or fold-down will occur if the channel configurations are standard (1, 2, 4, or 6 channels). If either the sample source or PlugIn output channel configurations are non-standard, then a simple re-mapping will occur. If there are less source sample channels than output channels, the remaining output channels will be silent. If there are more source sample channels than output channels, the left over source sample channels will be dropped.  To determine the number of channels in an SndPlayer asset at runtime you can use the static member function SndPlayer::GetFileInfo()
  A small amount of memory is set aside for each possible request. Setting maxRequests to a smaller number is therefore more efficient.
  Although console platforms will only ever let a 5.1 mode be set with SETMODE in the Dac, the GETMODE attribute will attempt to report the output mode setting as configured by the user in their console's set up screen.

  For 5.1 configurations, the center speaker level can also be controlled.
  For quad and 5.1 configurations, it is possible to over-ride the front and rear speaker geometry. By default the front speakers are set 45 degrees from front center, and the rear speakers are 135 degrees from front center (the front and rear speakers are equidistant in this configuration. The equidistant relationship provides some nice benefits when rotating multi-channel content around in circle, as it means a stereo image with 90 degree separation can be rotated to emanate from any two speakers without bleeding of the image.
  For stereo configurations, the speakers will always be placed 90 degrees from front center. This is not adjustable.
  For the PS3 setting stereo output mode will instruct RWAC to only output plain stereo when the OSD audio configuration is also set to stereo mode. This is the default output mode when the OSD is set to stereo. The 5.1 signal is folded down by dropping the LFE, dropping the surrounds by 3 dB and mixing them with the fronts, and then mixing the center channel in with the fronts at equal power (down 3 dB). The entire mix is then brought down an additional 3 dB to keep the signal from running too hot and clipping excessively where it wouldn't have in the 5.1 mix.

  If the specified output mode is not supported on the given platform/configuration, the next most logical output mode will be notched down to and selected automatically. This means the output mode could always be set to 5.1 if desired and the closest supported mode will be selected.
  Not expecting any arguments for option '%s'
  Note also that it is possible to explicitly set a start gain prior to a given fade. This can be achieved by specifying a fade of duration 0 with a start time of 0 and an end gain equal to the gain value desired. This will only take if both these parameters (fadeTime and startTime) are set to 0. One example of when this feature might be useful is if the user wants to ramp up a signal from 0 but the current gain of the fader is not 0, such as at plug-in startup (when the gain is set to 1). In this case, the user would specify two fades in succession, one to set the current gain to 0, and a second one to initiate the ramping up of the signal. Since the user may require that the initial gain setting (to 0 in this case) not delay the subsequent signal ramp up, both these gain fade events may be applied within the same lock (rw::audio::core::Lock()/Unlock()). As such, it is a special case, since otherwise the Gain Fader does not support queuing of gain fade events. It should also be noted that this feature should be used with extreme caution, since changing the gain value over a fade duration of 0 will introduce a click if the plug-in is passing a signal at the time the gain is changed. It should only be used if there is no signal being passed or it is known that the current signal amplitude is 0.
  OPTIONS
  On other console platforms, the modes supported will typically not change. 5.1 will be the only supported mode. Consoles will however reformat the 5.1 output to another format (mono, stereo, quad, ProLogic, Dolby Digital etc.). The reformatting is driven by settings on the console and is beyond the control of knowledge of RWAudioCore.
  On the PC platform, different PC's may support different modes based on the configuration of the PC software and hardware.
  On the PC platform, note that some output modes are not reliable, even if they are enumerated as supported (e.g. quad and 5.1). Although everything may appear to work correctly when interfacing with RWAudioCore, the user may experience crackling audio or missing channels for example. For that matter the quad and 5.1 modes will almost always be enumerated as supported even if the user only has two speakers. As the quad and 5.1 modes cannot be counted on to work on every machine, the game must provide an option to let the user switch between stereo, quad, and 5.1 modes. It is also a bonus to the user if an audio testing screen is included where a sound is played in turn on all channels. This way the user can determine if the quad or 5.1 modes will work properly for them in the game.
  On the PS3 the SETMODE event is respected in a specific situation in regards to desiring Dolby Pro Logic II output or plain stereo. The OSD and Sony libraries lack native support for PL2. Some game teams have requested PL2 support on PS3 so RWAC provides it via its own encoder. Unfortunately there is no way to select PL2 in the OSD as it doesn't support it. Switching between stereo and PL2 must be managed by calling SETMODE with either MODE_STEREO or MODE_PROLOGIC2. In this situation the SETMODE event acts more like a hint than an absolute setting. MODE_STEREO or MODE_PROLOGIC2 will only be respected if the OSD is set to output only 2 channels. Setting of other modes will be ignored, e.g. setting MODE_5POINT1 is meaningless as RWAC only respects the OSD when determining 5.1 output.
  On the Wii platform RWAC supports Pro Logic II and stereo, where RWAC defaults to PL2 mode.  PL2 encoding is provided by the Wii's Pro Logic II encoder.  Note that RWAC sets the Wii AX mode according to the output mode setting, so beware of this when the AX library is used outside of RWAC.
  On the other hand, it is suggested that most home theater configurations are set up with a geometry of 30/110 (front/rear). This would be another common geometry configuration to over-ride the panner to. The geometry of the panner can be over-ridden once in game code by calling rw::audio::core::Pan2D1::SetDefaultFrontSpeakerAngle() and rw::audio::core::Pan2D1::SetDefaultRearSpeakerAngle(). The geometry can also be over-ridden on a per panner instance basis by setting angles as parameters in the constructor to the panner.
  On the other hand, it is suggested that most home theater configurations are set up with a geometry of 30/110 (front/rear). This would be another common geometry configuration to over-ride the panner to. The geometry of the panner can be over-ridden once in game code by calling rw::audio::core::Pan2D::SetDefaultFrontSpeakerAngle() and rw::audio::core::Pan2D::SetDefaultRearSpeakerAngle(). The geometry can also be over-ridden on a per panner instance basis by setting angles as parameters in the constructor to the panner.
  Optionally force the loading of a planet.  Developed for testing procedural level generation.
  Options.txt
  POINTER_OPTIONAL
  Pitch values have a direct linear effect on the rate certain components (for example decoders) have to produce and process sample data. The components and plugins affected are only those upstream of the Resample plugin. The pitch can therefore have a direct effect on the amount of CPU consumed by these plugins. If pitch is set to 4.0, a decoder will use four times as much CPU than if the pitch was set to 1.0. Some components upstream of the resample may only be able to produce a certain amount of samples before an undesired side effect will occur. For example, the XMA decoder will not be able to produce enough samples to keep up with higher pitch values, and stuttering or CPU blocking will occur if stressed. It is generally recommended that pitch values above 2.0 not be used, however there area number of situations where pitch values above 2.0 would be fine. Lower source sample rates for example allow more headroom for pitching a sample up. If pitch values that are too high for the given components they will typically be clamped and printf type warnings to the console may be generated in debug configs.
  Pointer to the DSPSettings array

  Simulator/cSPUIGlobalOptions
  Since the ReverbIR1 PlugIn will tend to be used as a global effect, it is configured so that it will return nothing when inactive. In other words, the voice that the ReverbIR1 PlugIn resides in will not produce audio output to the voice(s) it feeds in this case. Normally, a separate parallel path would exist that persists even when the reverb is off, providing the dry, non-reverberated audio signal.
  Since the ReverbModel1 PlugIn will tend to be used as a global effect, it is configured so that it will return nothing when turned off (ATTRIBUTE_SETREVERBTIME = 0). In other words, the voice that the ReverbModel1 PlugIn resides in will not produce audio output to the voice(s) it feeds in this case. Normally, a separate parallel path would exist that persists even when the reverb is off, providing the dry, non-reverberated audio signal. 
  SndPlayer1 is one of the few PlugIns that supports non-standard channel configurations (up to 64). The Route PlugIn can be used to bus selections of channels to SubMix PlugIns, which can be useful for efficiently streaming layers of interactive music or ambience. For example, a stream file could be authored that contains 4 intensities of stereo crowd ambience. The 8 channel SndPlayer1 output can be routed into 4 stereo SubMixes, and then each SubMix Voice can vary its volume appropriately to cross-fade between the 4 intensities. This approach may use more CPU time than other approaches, but streaming a single 64 kilobyte/s stream will be far easier on a DVD than streaming 4 16 kilobyte/s streams. In most cases DVD bandwidth will be far more scarce than CPU bandwidth.
  Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Internet Settings
  Statistics are disabled, please enable them in hkbase/config/hkConfigMemoryStats.h
  SymGetOptions
  SymSetOptions
  The Pan3D plug-in allows for spatializing a mono signal over a 5.1 channel configuration. This plug-in is intended for use with the RWAudio3D library. RWAudio3D is responsible for producing a set of parameters or DSP coefficients which is to be used by Pan3D in processing the audio input signal.
  The Pan3D plug-in consists of a multi-tap delay line, a number of filters, and a number of Vector Base Amplitude Panning (VBAP) panners. The length of the delay line is determined by the constructor parameter maxDelaySeconds, and the number of read taps is determined by the number of reflections (a parameter in the DSPSettings struct) plus 1 for the direct path. The positions of the read taps are determined by the delay values of the direct and reflected paths. The input audio signal is first read either directly from a tap, or via interpolation in the case of a changing delay and corresponding Doppler shift. It then passes through a low pass filter, followed by a low shelving filter in the case of the direct path only, and finally into an improved VBAP panner. The low pass filter simulates air and surface absorption effects while the low shelving filter models the proximity effect. The VBAP panner maps the direct path azimuth to gain values for each of the 5 main channels in the 5.1 setup to allow for a smooth panning transition when the sound emitter is very close to the listener. The reflected paths use 2 channel panning on the assumption that reflections are normally far away, thereby reducing the amount of memory needed.
  The ReverbIR1 plug-in provides an efficient method for applying the reverberation characteristic of a given space to the game audio signal. This is achieved by performing the fast convolution of the signal with the impulse response (captured or created) of a given reverberant space. The efficient implementation is achieved by performing the convolution in the frequency domain, after having first sectioned the impulse response into smaller pieces in order to minimize delay and memory usage. To apply a given impulse response, a given impulse response file (such as say a stereo .wav file that contains the IR) must first be converted to an irf file (via SIMEX/SX). This file must be at the same sample rate as the output mode used by the game. The response is then applied to the signal via the EVENT_APPLYREVERB event. While the PlugIn has been designed for efficient operation, it does still require considerable resources, especially memory. To help mitigate this, memory usage can be traded off against the high frequency content of the reverberated signal retained. This is specified when creating the impulse response file (irf) via the SIMEX/SX parameter percentHighFrequencyCut. Reductions in memory usage will be nearly proportional to the numberspecified (i.e., if 50.0 is specified, memory usage will go down by close to half). To also minimize cycle usage, the SIMEX/SX parameter samplesPerFFTBlock allows for specification of the FFT block (segment)size. Larger sizes mean more efficient processing, but also add delay. Sizes used by the FFT must be a power of 2, so any sizes specified that aren't will be forced to a power of 2. The ReverbIR1 plug-in supports several channel-impulse response file configurations. 1:1 source to output channel mappings (1, 2, 4, or 6 channels) are supported, where the impulse response file is either mono, or it is comprised of the same number of channels as the signal input. Also, a 1:n source to output channel configuration is supported (1, 2, 4, or 6 output channels), where the impulse response file is comprised of n channels.
  The matrixpanner plug-in is used to place a mono sound source on multiple speakers, where each speaker has a separate gain controller.  The objective is to create a sense of placement relative to the game player when playing sounds. The gain levels should be specified from 0.0 to 1.0.  Negative values are allowed to invert the signal.  At present, the only configurations supported are 1X2, 1X4 and 1X6. 
  The other contruction parameter that has a great bearing on the amount of processor used is the Efficiency setting. This setting also relates to how the algorithm that finds the peak correlation points operates. Specifically, the algorithm first looks at a coarse granularity for the peak correlation point (within the correlation window), and then refines this peak location and value by moving at single sample granularity towards the true peak by moving in the direction of positive slope. The Efficiency setting, which ranges from a minimum of 1 (EFFICIENCY_1) to a maximum of 10 (EFFICIENCY_10), sets the granularity of the coarse search. The default setting of EFFICIENCY_6 corresponds to a coarse granularity of 16 samples, which seems to provide a good balance between efficiency and audio quality for most signals.
  The start time can be used to determine when the sine wave will begin generating output. The resolution of the start time is sample accurate.
  This attribute allows for the specification of the time in seconds over which the compressor reaches full compression, given that the mean magnitude of the input has exceeded the threshold. The punchiness characteristic of some compressors can be achieved by setting a relatively longer attack time ( > 1 second).
  This attribute specifies the size of the reverberant space via the setting of the average linear dimension of that space (in meters). The simplest example is that of a square room, in which case the space size would be specified as the distance between opposite walls. More complex geometries should be estimated by determining the average distance between pairs of opposing reflecting surfaces. 
  This event results in the DSPSettings object location being passed to Pan3D. The object is also copied to local memory owned by the Pan3D. The reason for copying the DSPSettings object to Pan3D local memory is to minimize the time duration during which the applicationcreating and filling in the DSPSettings object (such as RWAudio3D) and Pan3D are accessingthe same memory space. Given this approach, the only time during which a conflict could occur is during this event, when the data of DSPSettings is being copied over. As such, it is essential that the RWAC lock (rw::audio::core::System::Lock()) be acquired by the application whenever it is modifying the DSPSettings object. In the case of RWAudio3D, for example, this would be whenever the application calls the Calculate() method of that package to update the DSPSettings object values. 
  This mode can only be set via EVENT_SETMODE on the PS3 and will only be respected when the PS3 OSD audio configuration is set to a stereo output mode.
  This output parameter is pointed to a C string containing an end-user friendly name for the given output mode specified. This string may be used in option screens for example to enforce a consistent output mode naming across titles.
  This parameter is a pointer to a DSPSettings object that contains all the direct and reflected path details necessary for Pan3D to render the audio input to a 3D space. Typically the DSPSetting object will have been created by a higher level 3D audio application such as RWAudio3D. The object pointed to must be minimally aligned to 16 bytes to facilitate successful DMA transfers (PS3) and efficient processing and memory handling on all platforms. 
  This parameter should point to the seek table file (.sek). loaded into memory.  This parameter is optional unless a seek time has been specified.
  This value sets the correlation peak detection algorithm coarse correlation step size or granularity to 1 sample. This means that a coarse correlation is done for every sample offset. It is the least efficient setting, but guarantees that the optimal cut/loop point will always be found.

  When a mode other than 5.1 is set, the Dac plugin will perform an industry standard fold-down to the quad, stereo or mono output mode. Even if the Dac is not performing the fold-down, the PC or console platform might be performing a fold-down on its end. Hard clipping can be introduced during the fold-down if the signal coming into the fold-down is loud. Although the hard clipping should not be as audible a problem as it might sound, it can be avoided by reducing the volume of the 5.1 signal before it goes into the Dac plugin. If a signal is normalized to full scale (+/- 1.0) before it enters the Dac plugin by a plugin such as a compressor (strongly suggested), then when folding down to quad reducing the signal to 58% (-4.7 dB) of it's original level will guarantee no hard clipping will occur. When folding down to stereo, 37% (-8.6 dB) will guarantee no clipping. In reality, hard clipping noise is not as noticeable as one might think, and it may also be difficult drive the 5.1 signal to produce the extreme configuration required that will give a maximum peak after fold-down. For this reason it is never suggested that the volume be reduced to entirely reduce clipping (because a lot of volume will be lost), but instead find a happy medium through common sense and experimentation (e.g. somewhere between 0 and -6 dB).

  A PS3 game that wishes to support PL2 is faced with one of two choices on how to know when to enable it. The game code could have a front end menu option to allow the user to select between stereo and PL2. This is however very ugly to the end user as they now have to configure audio output in more than one place. The option is also confusing as it doesn't force stereo or PL2 mode if the OSD is set to a 5.1 or 7.1 output mode. For these reasons a menu option is not recommended. The saner alternative is to decide up front if your game will always output either plain stereo or PL2. This would probably be the audio director's choice based on what they prefer.

  On the Wii, the Dac sends its software mixed output through AX AuxB callback when the Dac is started.  The Dac upon creation will initialize the Wii AI and AX libraries by calling AIInit() and AXInit().  This means that the Dac will also be shutting down AI and AX by calling AIReset() and AXQuit() during Dac release to ensure proper matching calls.  Games that use AI and AX outside of RWAC should beware of these calls made in the Dac release (i.e. when releasing the voice with the Dac plugin or when releasing the RWAC System.  Note that stopping the Dac does not shutdown the AI and AX).  In the case of the Wii Home Menu, the game code should pause the master voice before entering the Wii Home Menu.

    Return to normal
   Another side effect of not performing pitch changes on a signal at the actual location desired is that frequency sensitive plugins (for example a low pass filter) will not react the same way as if the signal had actually been pitch changed. For example, a voice might have desired a situation where a low pass filter set the signal to  100 Hertz, then a doppler effect was applied through a pitch control. Imagine the doppler was such that the sample was traveling away from the listener to the point where the pitch would normally be halved. If the pitch change was actually made on the signal there, the frequency spectrum would be shifted from 0..100 Hertz to 0..50 Hertz. As RenderWare Audio Core doesn't allow pitch control at these points, the controlling layers of RenderWare Audio Core would have to compensate instead if a more accurate representation of frequency spectrum manipulation due to Doppler and similar pitch changes is desired. In this case, any frequency sensitive plugin could have its frequency control attribute scaled by the virtual pitch requests that are desired downstream of it. The low pass filter cutoff frequency in our above example would be multiplied by 0.5 and end up at  50 Hertz instead of 100 Hertz.

  =The Limiter1 plug-in provides a means for limiting the signal level.  When the mean magnitude of the signal exceeds some settable threshold, a gain term is applied to the signal to reduce the overall amplitude. This gain will cut in quickly, over a fixed attack time of 50 milliseconds (TBD if we need to change this). The gain will be fully released when the mean magnitude is below the threshold (including hysteresis) for a user specified release time. The gain amount is based on a fixed ratio of 10:1, whereby a 10 dB change in input signal level will result in a 1 dB change in output signal level. The gain is derived on a per channel basis, or can be based on the maximum mean magnitude channel across all channels. It should be noted that since the limiter threshold must be exceeded by the signal mean magnitude (via a lowpass filter with very low cutoff), in order to ensure that signal peaks that far exceed the mean are also compressed, a threshold that is much lower than those peaks must be selected. This might require a certain amount of experimentation. 
  >LineColorMiddle
  >LineColorTop
  ?ATTRIBUTE_GETSAMPLEPOSITION
  ?Highpass Filter State
  ?Mixer Lowpass Filter State
  ?Pitch is specified in linear units. A pitch of 1.0 will pass the signal through without modification, a value of 0.5 will drop the pitch an octave, and a value of 2.0 would increase the pitch by an octave.
  ?The BandPassIir2 plug-in allows for the relatively inexpensive band pass filtering of a signal. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a transition band roll-off of at least 12 dB per octave.The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear; though the degree of non-linearity in the pass band is not severe. Filter stability is assured by clamping the cutoff frequency (internally) to always be BETWEEN zero Hertz and the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate), and the bandwidth to be greater than zero. 
  ?The HighPassFir64 plug-in is a high quality linear phase highpass filter with excellent isolation. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a minimum of 50 dB stop band attenuation with a very narrow transition bandwidth in between.The filter uses only feed-forward elements, and as such can be classified as a Finite Impulse Response (FIR) filter. The filter is of order 64, utilizing 64 delay elements, and possessing the same number of zeros in its transfer function. Given its FIR nature, the phase response of the filter is linear, and the filter is unconditionally stable.
  ?The LowPassFir64 plug-in is a high quality linear phase lowpass filter with excellent isolation. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a minimum of 50 dB stop band attenuation with a very narrow transition bandwidth in between.The filter uses only feed-forward elements, and as such can be classified as a Finite Impulse Response (FIR) filter. The filter is of order 64, utilizing 64 delay elements, and possessing the same number of zeros in its transfer function. Given its FIR nature, the phase response of the filter is linear, and the filter is unconditionally stable.
  ?The LowPassIir2 plug-in allows for the relatively inexpensive low pass filtering of a signal. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a transition band roll-off of at least 12 dB per octave.The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear; though the degree of non-linearity in the pass band is not severe. Filter stability is assured by clamping the cutoff frequency (internally) to always be BETWEEN zero Hertz and the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate).
  ?The pan2d plug-in allows for panning of a signal within a unit circle, with speakers pre-positioned at fixed locations on the edge or the circle according to the number of output channels. The power will be distributed between the speakers based on an azimuth and radius relative to the listeners position at the center of the circle.
  A PS3 game that wishes to support PL2 is faced with one of two choices on how to know when to enable it. The game code could have a front end menu option to allow the user to select between stereo and PL2. This is however very ugly to the end user as they now have to configure audio output in more than one place. The option is also confusing as it doesn't force stereo or PL2 mode if the OSD is set to a 5.1 or 7.1 output mode. For these reasons a menu option is not recommended. The saner alternative is to decide up front if your game will always output either plain stereo or PL2. This would probably be the audio director's choice based on what they prefer.
  A value of 0.0 will stop playback entirely. Note that in this case the sample at the current playback position will be repeated until the pitch is set to anything but 0.0. This can create a DC offset when the resulting signal is mixed in with other audio, which can in turn cause nasty clipping and other distortion. If the pitch is set to 0.0, then gain for the voice should also be set to 0.0 to avoid this DC offset.

  Controls the position of the sound within the unit circle. A radius of one positions the sound on the circle and provides the sharpest results.   Defines the maximum delay length to be allocated for a Pan3D instance. The delay line is used for simulating the delay effect of the direct sound path from the sound source, the variously delayed reflections off walls and other surfaces, and the Doppler effect. A larger value normally allows the Pan3D instance to accommodate more reflections. If the size of the room that the Pan3D instance is simulating is large, maxDelaySeconds will also need to be large in order to enable hearing of at least all the first order reflections. A general usage guideline is to set maxDelaySeconds to the size of room that Pan3D is simulating (converted to seconds based on the speed of sound), within the constraints of available memory.

  Enumeration indicating the normalization technique to use when normalizing between input channels. 'UNIT' implies that normalization only occurs per channel, and that the contributions of individual input channels are added. 'NUM_INPUT_CHANNELS' implies that the input channels contain correlated wave-forms, and that the sum of contributions from individual input channels is scaled by 1/N, where N is the number of channels. 'SQRT_NUM_INPUT_CHANNELS' implies that the input channels contain uncorrelated wave-forms, and that the sum of contributions from individual input channels is scaled by 1/sqrt(N), where N is the number of channels.

  For looped samples, the position will move back to the starting loop point each time the loop section is played.
  For multi-channel inputs, controls the distance of the input channels from the sound location (the position described by 'Pan Angle' and 'Pan Distance'). Input channels use the same angular orientation as the speakers (controlled by 'Front Angle' and 'Rear Angle'), with the distance from the sound location controlled by 'Pan Size'.
  For the PS3 setting stereo output mode will instruct RWAC to only output plain stereo when the OSD audio configuration is also set to stereo mode. This is the default output mode when the OSD is set to stereo. The 5.1 signal is folded down by dropping the LFE, dropping the surrounds by 3 dB and mixing them with the fronts, and then mixing the center channel in with the fronts at equal power (down 3 dB). The entire mix is then brought down an additional 3 dB to keep the signal from running too hot and clipping excessively where it wouldn't have in the 5.1 mix.
  For the Wii, stereo mode will instruct RWAC to output stereo with the same 5.1 signal fold-down level as described for PS3.
  Gain is specified in linear units. A gain of 1.0 will pass the signal through without modification, a value of 0.0 will silence the signal, and a value of 2.0 would double the amplitude of the signal. A value of -0.5 would reduce volume to half and invert the phase of the signal.
  Games should default their output mode to stereo on the PC as it is the most compatible output mode (this is the RWAC default as well). The 5.1 signal is folded down by dropping the LFE, mixing the surrounds to the fronts, and then mixing the center channel in with the fronts at equal power (down 3 dB). Note this is not the same fold-down levels used on the PS3 and the PC will be changed to work like the PS3 fold-down in the future.

  If playback needs to be scheduled in the near future (e.g. the next few hundred milliseconds), then the Play event should be called from an RWAC timer callback. This will ensure that your code is called in a timely manner before the system time has a chance to pass you by.
  If the length and current sample position are known, the time remaining to play in the sample can be derived from them.
  If the sample position value will be used for attempting near sample accurate synchronization, the documentation for rw::audio::core::System::AddTimerClient() should be referred to for details on how to achieve it.
