  Latency becomes a large factor when trying to synchronize other game concepts such as animation or video to audio. If the latency is not taken into account and the Dac is adding 300 milliseconds of latency, then the video will be out of sync with the audio by 300 milliseconds. This will make for an unpleasant end user experience. Game code and other systems should get around this by compensating for the latency introduced. If the latency was 300 milliseconds, then 300 milliseconds should be subtracted from the current playback position of a sample being played which is being synchronized. The current latency must be subtracted from the sample playback position each time it is polled, otherwise drift could occur as latency changes. This will keep the audio and video in sync.

  NormalizationMode
  NormalizationMode specifies how the output channel gains are scaled in the case of multiple input channels.
  Note also that when the ATTRIBUTE_STRETCHRATIO is set exactly to 1, the plugin switches to a mode where it simply passes through the input data without any time stretch processing. This eliminates the CPU load normally associated with the time stretcher, and allows the game code to save cycles when they are not needed.
  Note that although the startTime is specified with very high precision that allows sample accurate results, rounding errors may occur in some cases that could cause the sample number to be off by 1 sample from where the desired results are. This would most often be a problem if attempting to start a new sample relative to another sample that is playing and being pitched, as the other sample position will be located at a fractional position within a single sampling point, due to the nature of resampling. If possible, the stitching mechanism provided by queueing samples one after another in a single SndPlayer1 instance should be used if seamless stitching is required.
  Note the file path must be appropriately specified for use with the ANSI fwrite() API on the platform being used. PS3 for example should specify the /app_home/ device or similar. Xbox 360 should specify a valid path on the D: drive or similar.
  OBJECTTYPE_SHADERSINK
  On the PC and Xbox 360 platforms the impulse response file must be 16 byte aligned in memory. On the PS3 128 byte alignment is required. This is necessary to allow efficient execution of the plugin.
  On the PC platform, it is possible that no sound card is installed on a machine or the sound system is not working for some reason. When this happens, the Dac provides functionality to "pretend" that it is operating normally as if a sound card is present. RWAudioCore will act as if nothing is wrong. This functionality is provided so that game programmers can handle the missing sound system with ease. The alternative is that special code must be inserted all over the place to handle RWAudioCore API functions not working as expected.
  On the PS3 it is RWAC itself that encodes Dolby Pro Logic II signals. The encode is performed on the final 5.1 mix and is a proper PL2 encode and fold-down algorithm as recommended by Dolby. The overall mix level is brought down an additional 3 dB the same way the stereo fold-down brings it down to prevent the 5.1 signal from getting too hot when carried over 2 channels. This also ensure level matching with the stereo mode.
  On the PS3 the SETMODE event is respected in a specific situation in regards to desiring Dolby Pro Logic II output or plain stereo. The OSD and Sony libraries lack native support for PL2. Some game teams have requested PL2 support on PS3 so RWAC provides it via its own encoder. Unfortunately there is no way to select PL2 in the OSD as it doesn't support it. Switching between stereo and PL2 must be managed by calling SETMODE with either MODE_STEREO or MODE_PROLOGIC2. In this situation the SETMODE event acts more like a hint than an absolute setting. MODE_STEREO or MODE_PROLOGIC2 will only be respected if the OSD is set to output only 2 channels. Setting of other modes will be ignored, e.g. setting MODE_5POINT1 is meaningless as RWAC only respects the OSD when determining 5.1 output.
  On the PS3, the Dac outputs audio via Sony's libaudio library. One of the libaudio ports is used, leaving three others free for other audio middleware to coexist peacefully with RWAC. As a convenience, the Dac upon creation will initialize the PS3 audio system with cellAudioInit() if it has not already been initialized by other code game code or audio middleware. In most cases it is likely RWAC will be the only low level audio middleware being used so game code will not have to deal with the PS3 audio system at all. If cellAudioInit() is initialized by the Dac then it will also call cellAudioQuit() when it is released, otherwise it will not be called. This means if cellAudioInit() has already been called before the Dac is created, the Dac should be released before cellAudioQuit() is called to ensure proper matching calls to cellAudioInit() and cellAudioQuit().

  Since the ReverbIR1 PlugIn will tend to be used as a global effect, it is configured so that it will return nothing when inactive.   The BandPassFir64 plug-in is a high quality linear phase bandpass filter with excellent isolation. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a minimum of 50 dB stop band attenuation with a very narrow transition bandwidth in between.The filter uses only feed-forward elements, and as such can be classified as a Finite Impulse Response (FIR) filter. The filter is of order 64, utilizing 64 delay elements, and possessing the same number of zeros in its transfer function. Given its FIR nature, the phase response of the filter is linear, and the filter is unconditionally stable.
  The Compressor1 plug-in provides a means for dynamic range reduction of the signal. When the mean magnitude of the signal exceeds some settable threshold, a gain term is applied to the signal to reduce the overall amplitude. This gain will only cut in fully after a specified attack time, and conversely, will only be fully released when the mean magnitude is below the threshold (including hysteresis) for a specified release time. The gain amount is based on a settable ratio, and can be derived on a per channel basis, or can be based on the maximum mean magnitude channel across all channels. The punchiness characteristic of some compressors can be achieved by setting a relatively longer attack time ( > 1 second), while the sustain of certain compressors can be achieved by a longer release time. It should be noted that since the compressor threshold must be exceeded by the signal mean magnitude (via a lowpass filter with very low cutoff), in order to ensure that signal peaks that far exceed the mean are also compressed, a threshold that is much lower than those peaks must be selected. This might require a certain amount of experimentation. 
  The HighPassButterworth plug-in provides users with a high pass version of the classic analog IIR Butterworth filter. The Butterworth filter frequency response is referred to as being maximally flat in the passband. What this means in practice is that the pass band frequency response of the filter is very flat, not exhibiting the ripples seen in other filter types, such as the Butterworth's analog cousins, the Chebyshev and Elliptic filters. Also, the frequency response is referred to as being monotonic in the pass and stop bands, which means that as frequency decreases, the amplitude of the response never increases, but rather will either stay the same or decrease. The filter gain in the passband is normalized to 0 dB. Near the filter cutoff frequency (3 dB point), the rolloff of the filter will be around 6 dB per octave per order or pole (see ATTRIBUTE_SETFREQUENCY description). As such, users can select the filter order based on their needs. For example, if the use case requires a filter with maximum rolloff in order to cut out any frequencies below a certain value, the maximum filter order should be chosen. On the other hand if the use case requires a very gentle rolloff in order to model some real world physical acoustic phenomenon, then perhaps selecting a lower order makes the most sense. These filters are guaranteed to be stable, something that is inherent in the digital Butterworth filter design process. 
  The HighPassIir2 plug-in allows for the relatively inexpensive high pass filtering of a signal. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a transition band roll-off of at least 12 dB per octave.The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear; though the degree of non-linearity in the pass band is not severe. Filter stability is assured by clamping the cutoff frequency (internally) to always be BETWEEN zero Hertz and the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate).
  The LowPassButterworth plug-in provides users with a low pass version of the classic analog IIR Butterworth filter. The Butterworth filter frequency response is referred to as being maximally flat in the passband. What this means in practice is that the pass band frequency response of the filter is very flat, not exhibiting the ripples seen in other filter types, such as the Butterworth's analog cousins, the Chebyshev and Elliptic filters. Also, the frequency response is referred to as being monotonic in the pass and stop bands, which means that as frequency increases, the amplitude of the response never increases, but rather will either stay the same or decrease. The filter gain in the passband is normalized to 0 dB. Near the filter cutoff frequency (3 dB point), the rolloff of the filter will be around 6 dB per octave per order or pole (see ATTRIBUTE_SETFREQUENCY description). As such, users can select the filter order based on their needs. For example, if the use case requires a filter with maximum rolloff in order to cut out any frequencies beyond a certain value, the maximum filter order should be chosen. On the other hand if the use case requires a very gentle rolloff in order to model some real world physical acoustic phenomenon (such as for example occlusion), then perhaps selecting a lower order (such as 1) makes the most sense. These filters are guaranteed to be stable, somethingthat is inherent in the digital Butterworth filter design process. 
  The Pan3D plug-in consists of a multi-tap delay line, a number of filters, and a number of Vector Base Amplitude Panning (VBAP) panners. The length of the delay line is determined by the constructor parameter maxDelaySeconds, and the number of read taps is determined by the number of reflections (a parameter in the DSPSettings struct) plus 1 for the direct path. The positions of the read taps are determined by the delay values of the direct and reflected paths. The input audio signal is first read either directly from a tap, or via interpolation in the case of a changing delay and corresponding Doppler shift. It then passes through a low pass filter, followed by a low shelving filter in the case of the direct path only, and finally into an improved VBAP panner. The low pass filter simulates air and surface absorption effects while the low shelving filter models the proximity effect. The VBAP panner maps the direct path azimuth to gain values for each of the 5 main channels in the 5.1 setup to allow for a smooth panning transition when the sound emitter is very close to the listener. The reflected paths use 2 channel panning on the assumption that reflections are normally far away, thereby reducing the amount of memory needed.
  The ReverbModel1 plug-in is a parameterized model-based reverberator that allows some user control of the size and characteristics of a reverberant space.  Each instance takes in a single (mono) source, and fans out a reverberated spatialized signal to the selected number of output channels (except the LFE in 6 channel case). The reverberator is comprised of 6 parallel comb filters followed by a series allpass filter. A degree of spatialization is achieved by using slightly different parameters for the allpass on one output channel versus another. Control over the reverb is achieved via 3 parameters: the reverb space size, reverberation time, and brightness. Each of these is associated with an attribute, and described there.
  The SubMix PlugIn behaves like a mix bin. Any number of voices may connect their output via a Send or Route PlugIn to a SubMix. The SubMix will mix the output of these and pass the result down the voice it is plugged into.
  The SubMix is a "source" PlugIn, and it must be inserted alone at position 0 on the voice. The SubMix's input channels must be set to 0, and the output channels must set to either 1, 2, 4, or 6 channels.
  The current implementation of the Pan3D plug-in uses the IIR2 class for both the low pass and the low shelving filter, and uses CopyWithGainRamp() and MixWithGainRamp() to apply the gain matrices. The delay line interpolation is implemented as a two-point linear interpolation. In the case where the sound source is located at such a great distance that the size of the delay line required exceeds maxDelaySeconds and the user still requires the Doppler effect, the Pan3D plug-in will use a time-invariant implementation of a pitch shifter which is based on crossfading two modulated delay lines. 
  The pan2d plug-in allows for panning of a signal, with speakers pre-positioned at fixed locations on the edge of the circle according to the number of output channels. The power will be distributed between the speakers based on an panangle, pandistance, pansize, and pantwist relative to the listeners position at the center of the circle.
  The route plug-in allows sending specific channels of a source voice to the same or different channels in a submix. In addition to sending these channels to the designated submix, all channels flowing into the plug-in also pass through it to the next plug-in in the voice (if there is one). This allows for further processing of the source voice, such as the routing of a different subset of the channels with subsequent route plug-ins. 
  This attribute allows for specification of how the compressor will behave in a multi-channel case (the norm). If grouped operation is specified, the channel with the lowest current gain will have its gain applied to all channels. This will normally be the channel with the highest signal level, but given attack and release times, and the time constant of the lowpass filter doing the averaging, it could occasionally be a different channel. Use of this mode allows for the preservation of the multi-channel image, since whenever gain is applied, the gain value applied will be the same across all channels. This mode is the default. If independent mode is selected, each channel will use a gain based solely on that channels mean magnitude. This mode might be useful if the user simply wants to compress when it is warrented, on a channel-by-channel basis. 
  This attribute allows for specification of how the limiter will behave in a multi-channel case (the norm). If grouped operation is specified, the channel with the lowest current gain will have its gain applied to all channels. This will normally be the channel with the highest signal level, but given attack and release times, and the time constant of the lowpass filter doing the averaging, it could occasionally be a different channel. Use of this mode allows for the preservation of the multi-channel image, since whenever gain is applied, the gain value applied will be the same across all channels. This mode is the default. If independent mode is selected, each channel will use a gain based solely on that channels mean magnitude. This mode might be useful if the user simply wants to compress when it is warrented, on a channel-by-channel basis. 
  This attribute allows for the adjustment of the perceived brightness of the reverb. By increasing the brightness, the reverberated signal will retain proportionally higher frequency components. This is achieved by increasing the cutoff frequency of each lowpass filter in the comb filter feedback loop. By selecting a brightness of 1 (the default), the frequency content of the reverb will be matched with that defined by the model. It is recommended that this default normally be used. However, tweaking of this parameter may be useful to emulate effects such as the humidity of the environment (i.e., higher humidity corresponds to a less bright sound, since the moisture absorbs higher frequencies). 
  This attribute indicates the current playback position of the actively playing sample. The units are in seconds.
  This mode can only be set via EVENT_SETMODE on the PS3 and will only be respected when the PS3 OSD audio configuration is set to a stereo output mode.
  This parameter is a pointer to a DSPSettings object that contains all the direct and reflected path details necessary for Pan3D to render the audio input to a 3D space. Typically the DSPSetting object will have been created by a higher level 3D audio application such as RWAudio3D. The object pointed to must be minimally aligned to 16 bytes to facilitate successful DMA transfers (PS3) and efficient processing and memory handling on all platforms. 
  This value indicates that normalization only occurs per channel. Contributions of individual channels are added.

  Unlike the Xbox 360, the PS3 does not automatically encode audio into the format the user has selected in the OSD.RWAC looks at what audio output modes are set up in the OSD and selects the one it feels will yield the highest quality results. The selection criteria first attempts 8 channel modes, 6 channel modes, then 2 channel modes. Within each channel selection it will attempt the encoding method that introduces the least distortion into the signal. Given only some of the OSD modes are supported this effectively results in the order of selection being HDMI 7.1, HDMI 5.1, HDMI or optical Dolby Digital 5.1, then HDMI or optical or analog 2 channel. Note at this time there is a known issue with HDMI 7.1 output so even if this is the only mode selected in the OSD you will actually get HDMI 5.1 as 5.1 works.
  Unsupported color conversion request
  VBase Color
  VRegion Color
  When a mode other than 5.1 is set, the Dac plugin will perform an industry standard fold-down to the quad, stereo or mono output mode. Even if the Dac is not performing the fold-down, the PC or console platform might be performing a fold-down on its end. Hard clipping can be introduced during the fold-down if the signal coming into the fold-down is loud. Although the hard clipping should not be as audible a problem as it might sound, it can be avoided by reducing the volume of the 5.1 signal before it goes into the Dac plugin. If a signal is normalized to full scale (+/- 1.0) before it enters the Dac plugin by a plugin such as a compressor (strongly suggested), then when folding down to quad reducing the signal to 58% (-4.7 dB) of it's original level will guarantee no hard clipping will occur. When folding down to stereo, 37% (-8.6 dB) will guarantee no clipping. In reality, hard clipping noise is not as noticeable as one might think, and it may also be difficult drive the 5.1 signal to produce the extreme configuration required that will give a maximum peak after fold-down. For this reason it is never suggested that the volume be reduced to entirely reduce clipping (because a lot of volume will be lost), but instead find a happy medium through common sense and experimentation (e.g. somewhere between 0 and -6 dB).
  When the PS3 is in PL2 mode a small amount of extra SPU cycles are used compared to stereo (~0.2%). This means your game should be probably tested in the most expensive output mode (currently PL2) to ensure performance is adequate. See the RWAC benchmarks for the latest SPU usage differences between stereo, PL2, and 5.1.

===== [AUDIO]  187 unique =====
   Another side effect of not performing pitch changes on a signal at the actual location desired is that frequency sensitive plugins (for example a low pass filter) will not react the same way as if the signal had actually been pitch changed. For example, a voice might have desired a situation where a low pass filter set the signal to  100 Hertz, then a doppler effect was applied through a pitch control. Imagine the doppler was such that the sample was traveling away from the listener to the point where the pitch would normally be halved. If the pitch change was actually made on the signal there, the frequency spectrum would be shifted from 0..100 Hertz to 0..50 Hertz. As RenderWare Audio Core doesn't allow pitch control at these points, the controlling layers of RenderWare Audio Core would have to compensate instead if a more accurate representation of frequency spectrum manipulation due to Doppler and similar pitch changes is desired. In this case, any frequency sensitive plugin could have its frequency control attribute scaled by the virtual pitch requests that are desired downstream of it. The low pass filter cutoff frequency in our above example would be multiplied by 0.5 and end up at  50 Hertz instead of 100 Hertz. 
   The PlayParams parameter must be used to inform the RawPuller what data to expect.  The data sample rate and number of channels must be those of the input data, and the vector to the callback function to feed input data must be provided. The optional pContext pointer can also be used, and will have the same value as the one found in the callback function.
   The sample data provided to pDest must be floats, in block format as described in \ref p_ChannelSupport "Channel Support". The nominal values for the sample data should be in the range of -1.0f..1.0f, which is the standard range used by all RWAudioCore PlugIns. Values outside this range will be clamped in the final mix stage if they are still out of range at that point, and values that are exceedingly large (for example +-100000) may trigger assertions as it is expected these values are not correct.
  =The Limiter1 plug-in provides a means for limiting the signal level.  When the mean magnitude of the signal exceeds some settable threshold, a gain term is applied to the signal to reduce the overall amplitude. This gain will cut in quickly, over a fixed attack time of 50 milliseconds (TBD if we need to change this). The gain will be fully released when the mean magnitude is below the threshold (including hysteresis) for a user specified release time. The gain amount is based on a fixed ratio of 10:1, whereby a 10 dB change in input signal level will result in a 1 dB change in output signal level. The gain is derived on a per channel basis, or can be based on the maximum mean magnitude channel across all channels. It should be noted that since the limiter threshold must be exceeded by the signal mean magnitude (via a lowpass filter with very low cutoff), in order to ensure that signal peaks that far exceed the mean are also compressed, a threshold that is much lower than those peaks must be selected. This might require a certain amount of experimentation.

  ?The BandPassIir2 plug-in allows for the relatively inexpensive band pass filtering of a signal. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a transition band roll-off of at least 12 dB per octave.The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear; though the degree of non-linearity in the pass band is not severe. Filter stability is assured by clamping the cutoff frequency (internally) to always be BETWEEN zero Hertz and the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate), and the bandwidth to be greater than zero. 
  ?The LowPassIir2 plug-in allows for the relatively inexpensive low pass filtering of a signal. The filter provides zero dB gain in the pass band, and a transition band roll-off of at least 12 dB per octave.The filter uses feed-forward and feed-back elements, and as such can be classified as an Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter. The filter is second order, with 2 non-trivial poles and zeros. Given its IIR nature, the phase response of the filter is non-linear; though the degree of non-linearity in the pass band is not severe. Filter stability is assured by clamping the cutoff frequency (internally) to always be BETWEEN zero Hertz and the Nyquist frequency (one half the sample rate).

  @?EAPd/Mixer
  A SndPlayer1 instance will always output a constant number of channels, but the source sample data may be of any channel configuration. If the source and output channels do not map 1:1, then a standard fold-up or fold-down will occur if the channel configurations are standard (1, 2, 4, or 6 channels). If either the sample source or PlugIn output channel configurations are non-standard, then a simple re-mapping will occur. If there are less source sample channels than output channels, the remaining output channels will be silent. If there are more source sample channels than output channels, the left over source sample channels will be dropped.  To determine the number of channels in an SndPlayer asset at runtime you can use the static member function SndPlayer::GetFileInfo()
  A send may be connected to a submix by specifying the name of the SubMix plugin instance it wishes to connect to. Note the mastering voice's submix name is "Master". To disconnect a send connected by name, specify a NULL pointer to ConnectByPointer.
  A send may be connected to a submix by specifying the pointer to the SubMix plugin instance.

  Also note that most impulse response files will have some gain associated with them. If this is significantly larger than one and the plugin input signal is also fairly large, the ReverbIR1 output will most likely get clipped in the mastering voice, causing distortion. This can be avoided by decreasing the send level into or out of the ReverbIR1 voice, or by reducing the level via a gain plugin within the ReverbIR1 voice. 
  Although console platforms will only ever let a 5.1 mode be set with SETMODE in the Dac, the GETMODE attribute will attempt to report the output mode setting as configured by the user in their console's set up screen.

  As the SampleCapture plug-in is of PLUGINTYPE_STANDARD, it cannot be inserted before such plug-ins as the SndPlayer, Rechannel, Resample, or TimeStretch.
  As this plugin is primarily meant for debugging, it is not recommended to use it in shipping games at this time, as it is not implemented in an efficient manner. The synchronous nature of the ANSI file writing calls used here makes it quite possible the RWAC audio thread will be stalled and a buffer underrun will occur (crackling audio). The chance of this happening is likely going to depend on what device is being written to.

  ChannelMode specifies how the compressor channel gain should be applied in the multi-channel case.
  ChannelMode specifies how the limiter channel gain should be applied in the multi-channel case.
  Defines the maximum delay length to be allocated for a Pan3D instance. The delay line is used for simulating the delay effect of the direct sound path from the sound source, the variously delayed reflections off walls and other surfaces, and the Doppler effect. A larger value normally allows the Pan3D instance to accommodate more reflections. If the size of the room that the Pan3D instance is simulating is large, maxDelaySeconds will also need to be large in order to enable hearing of at least all the first order reflections. A general usage guideline is to set maxDelaySeconds to the size of room that Pan3D is simulating (converted to seconds based on the speed of sound), within the constraints of available memory.

  EVENT_STOPREVERB
